Fault tolerance in para-line network topologies: theory and applications in smart systems
Authors: Sanaa Ahmed Bajri, Muhammad Ahmad, Muhammad Faheem, Muhammad Naveed Jafar, Alhanouf Alburaikan, Hamiden Abd El-Wahed Khalifa
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This study explores the fault-tolerant metric dimension (FTMD) of the para-line network, which is derived from the n-sunlet network, a significant category of networks created by subdividing and transforming cycle-based networks to simulate greater structural complexity. FTMD ensures robust vertex identification even when some nodes fail, a characteristic essential for fault-resilient systems like communication and sensor networks. By rigorously analyzing the structural attributes of these para-line networks, we demonstrate that the FTMD consistently behaves in a specific manner: it is equal to 3 when n=3 or 6, and 4 otherwise. Our approach includes constructive distance-vector analysis and combinatorial proofs to ensure the smallest possible size of the fault-tolerant resolving set (FTRS). These findings contribute both theoretically and practically, offering new perspectives on the development of resilient network topologies for practical applications such as IoT infrastructure, smart cities, and fault-tolerant distributed systems.

Introduction

The metric dimension (MD) of a network is a fundamental parameter in graph theory, introduced to uniquely identify all vertices of a network based on their distances from a selected set of vertices. This concept, formalized by Slater1 and Harary and Melter2, has applications in diverse areas such as robotics, where it aids in navigation and localization tasks; network security, where MD supports the detection of intrusions and vulnerabilities, and bioinformatics, where it helps analyze genetic and molecular structures3.

An extension of this concept, the FTMD, ensures that the unique identification of vertices remains intact even when a fixed number of vertices or edges fail. FTMD has proven essential in the design of resilient systems such as communication networks4, where uninterrupted operations are required despite failures; in wireless sensor networks5, where sensor failures are inevitable for real-time monitoring and data collection; and in disaster management6, where maintaining critical routes during infrastructure breakdowns is vital.

Among various network families, the n-sunlet network, a network formed by attaching a pendant vertex to each vertex of a cycle, has attracted significant attention. Its hierarchical and hybrid structure is useful in modeling hub-and-spoke communication networks and distributed computing architectures. Extending this structure, the para-line network of an n-sunlet network incorporates additional edges, creating a richer topology for investigating fault-tolerant properties.

While recent advancements in FTMD have yielded insights into families such as Cartesian product networks7, circulant networks8, and hierarchical networks, the FTMD of para-line networks derived from n-sunlet networks remain unexplored. This study seeks to address this gap by analyzing the FTMD of para-line networks of n-sunlet networks, providing exact values, bounds, and structural properties. The findings are expected to contribute significantly to the theoretical understanding of FTMD and to practical applications in network optimization and system reliability.

Previous work

The concept of MD in graph theory was first introduced by Slater1 through the notion of leaves of trees, which laid the foundation for vertex resolvability in networks. Following this, Harary and Melter2 formally defined the MD of a network, establishing its significance in structural network analysis. Chartrand et al.9 further advanced the field by formalizing the concept of resolvability and studying its applications across different network classes. Poisson and Zhang10 extended these concepts to unicyclic networks, characterizing their MD and contributing to the broader study of resolvability in networks, while Chartrand and Zhang11 provided a comprehensive survey on resolvability, highlighting its theoretical and practical implications.

Furthermore, many network families have been explored concerning their MD. For instance, Javaid et al.12 focused on regular networks with constant MD, while Imran et al.13 and Borchert and Gosselin14 analyzed the MD of circulant networks and Cayley hypernetworks, extending the applicability of metric-based network analysis. Additionally, the study in Ahmad et al.15 examined the edge locating numbers and metric size of coffee chemical structures to understand coffee molecules. In Koam16, examined the edge MD of some convex polytope structures and compares the results with previous resolvability parameter cardinalities. Pan et al.17 studied the double MD in convex polytopes, enhancing understanding of network resolvability.

The study of FTMD emerged as a crucial extension of classical resolvability, introduced in the work of Hernando et al.18. Their study examined robustness in networks under vertex failures, a concept further explored by Javaid et al.19 in the context of fault-tolerance in resolvability. Manuel et al.20 studied honeycomb networks, while Basak et al.21 examined FTMD in circulant networks. Saha22 investigated fault-tolerant resolvability in the cube of paths, contributing to structural fault analysis in networked systems. The problem of determining the MD and FTMD is computationally challenging, especially for general networks where it is NP-hard23.

Recent research in Simic et al.24 proposed an integer linear programming method for the FTMD problem, achieving exact solutions for grid networks and establishing a foundation for network analysis. Raza et al.25 later examined FTMD in interconnection networks, illustrating the enhancement of resilience through fault-tolerant resolving sets (FTRS). In a subsequent study, Raza et al.26 investigated extremal network structures, focusing on the influence of these resolving sets (RS) on connectivity. Somasundari and Raj27 explored FTMD in oxide interconnections, revealing its relevance to material science. Prabhu et al.7 analyzed twin vertices in fault-tolerant metric sets, underscoring their role in efficiency for multistage interconnection networks.

Voronov28 examined the FTMD of the king’s network, a crucial structure in computational geometry. Siddiqui et al.29 built on this by analyzing convex polytopes and their importance in computational topology. Sharma and Bhat30 studied double antiprism networks, highlighting fault tolerance in complex three-dimensional structures. Recently, Saha et al.8 offered a fresh perspective on circulant networks and their impact on FTMD. Raza et al.31 deepened the understanding of FTMD in higher-dimensional convex polytopes. Nadeem et al.32 explored FTMD in chemical network structures related to molecular stability and drug design. Similarly, Koam et al.33 studied hollow coronoid structures, linking fault-tolerant resolvability to advanced material science and nanotechnology.

In a recent research, Liu et al.34 examined the FTMD of wheel networks, revealing unique resolvability properties under faulty conditions, while Zheng et al.35 expanded this to generalized wheels and convex polytopes. Hayat et al.36 focused on FTMD in interconnection networks, emphasizing its importance for robustness and navigation. Ali et al.37 studied FTMD in multilevel networks, emphasizing its significance in hierarchical systems like transportation and communication. Guo et al.38 and Faheem & Zahid39 investigated FTMD in line networks, including dragon and kayak paddles, while Wang et al.40 analyzed the resilience of ladder networks.

In the study in Faheem et al.41, the authors demonstrate how subdivision impacts the resolvability of a network and propose efficient methods for constructing FTRSs. Estrado-Moreno et al.42,43 introduced k-metric resolvability as a basis for fault tolerance in RSs. More recently, Faheem et al.44 expanded the FTMD concept to edge-fault tolerance in networks, reflecting emerging trends in addressing both vertex and edge failures. In another study, Ahmad et al.45 investigated the FTMD in connected networks, creating computational methods and establishing stability and fluctuation bounds for trees and cycles amid structural changes. Recently, the authors in Faheem et al.46 derived MD and FTMD values for para-line prism networks, emphasizing their importance for navigation and robustness while discussing computational complexity and real-world applications.

Fault diagnosis and resolvability in network structures are essential for system reliability and efficiency. Multi-label classification methods are commonly used in fault diagnosis. Tan et al.47 compared multi-label techniques for diagnosing faults in marine machinery, highlighting the importance of simultaneous fault identification to enhance robustness. Dineva et al.48 employed multi-label classification for electrical machine faults, aligning with the work of Shariat et al.49, emphasize the importance of cross-layer scheduling in broadband wireless systems, reviewing strategies that enhance performance by integrating MAC and physical layer operations. Quamar and Nasir50 reviewed fault diagnosis and control in robotic manipulators, utilizing AI for enhanced fault detection. This body of research highlights the interdisciplinary nature of FTMD, bridging theoretical models with practical applications in various fields.

Research motivation

The motivation for studying the FTMD of the para-line network derived from the n-sunlet network stems from the necessity to grasp how modifications in the network’s structure influence resolvability in the presence of faults. Although FTMD has been studied for standard network families, its behavior in composite structures such as para-line networks have not been extensively investigated. This study addresses that gap by deriving exact FTMD values for a class of networks obtained through subdivision and line network operations. Though primarily theoretical, these findings provide valuable insights for the development of fault-tolerant systems in areas like sensor networks and interconnection configurations, where reliable identification and redundancy are crucial. The findings contribute to the broader combinatorial theory and provide a conceptual framework for modeling robustness in interconnected systems.

Main contribution

The primary contributions of this article are structured as follows: “Basic results” Section introduces the necessary preliminary definitions and results required to calculate the FTMD of the para network of the n-sunlet network. In “FTMD for the para-line network of n-sunlet network” Section, we present the FTMD values associated with the para network of the n-sunlet network. The “Discussion, computational analysis, and practical implications” Section offers a thorough discussion, encompassing an analysis of computational complexity and the practical implications of these results in real-world scenarios. Lastly, “Conclusion” Section concludes the study, emphasizing possible directions for future research and pointing out gaps in the current literature.

Basic results

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} represent the sets of edges (branches) and vertices (nodes) of a simple, connected, and undirected network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}(V({\mathbf {G}}), E({\mathbf {G}}))$$\end{document}. The degree of a node v, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\mathbf {G}}(v)$$\end{document}, refers to the number of edges incident to the node v. The distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v_{1},v_{2})$$\end{document} between two vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1},v_{2}\in V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} is defined as the number of edges on the shortest path connecting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{2}$$\end{document}. A vertex v is said to resolves a pair of vertices, t and u if the distances from t to v and from u to v are different, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(t,v)\ne d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(u,v)$$\end{document}. To effectively investigate the FTMD for para-line n-sunlet networks, here are several key definitions that provide foundational concepts that will not only clarify our understanding but also guide us in our investigation.

Definition 2.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W= \{w_{1}, w_{2},\ldots , w_{p}\} \subset V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}. The absolute difference code produces a p-vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(|d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v_{1}, w_{1})-d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v_{2}, w_{1})|,\ldots ,|d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v_{1}, w_{p})-d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v_{2}, w_{p})|)$$\end{document} for any vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}, v_{2} \in V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} in relation to W, which is denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((v_{1}, v_{2})|W)$$\end{document}.

Definition 2.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W = \{w_{1}, w_{2},\ldots , w_{p}\} \subset V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}. The tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v, w_{1}), d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v, w_{2}),\ldots , d_{{\mathbf {G}}}(v, w_{p})\big )$$\end{document} represents the distance code r(v|W) of order p for a vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(v_{1} | W) \ne r(v_{2} | W)$$\end{document} for any distinct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}, v_{2}\in V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}, then W is referred to as a RS of the network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document}. Additionally, W is termed the RS if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((v_{1}, v_{2})|W)$$\end{document} for each pair of distinct vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}, v_{2}\in V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} includes at least one non-zero element in their p-vector. The MD, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}, represents the smallest size of W.

Definition 2.3

A RS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime} = \{w^{\prime}_{1}, w^{\prime}_{2},\ldots , w^{\prime}_{p}\} \subset V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} is termed a FTRS, if the removal of any vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}$$\end{document} still results in a RS. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} known as an FTRS if, for every pair of distinct vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{1}, v_{2} \in V({\mathbf {G}}),$$\end{document} their absolute difference, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((v_{1}, v_{2})|W^{\prime})$$\end{document}, contain at least two nonzero entries in their p-vector. The FTMD, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}, is the minimum possible size of such a set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document}.

Definition 2.4

The line network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} of a given network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document} is constructed by representing the edges of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document} as vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}. Two vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} are connected if and only if their corresponding edges in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document} share a common endpoint.

Definition 2.5

A subdivision network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} of a given network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document} is obtained by inserting a new vertex into each edge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document}, effectively dividing each edge into two segments.

Definition 2.6

For a given network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document}, its subdivision network is denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}. The line network of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S({\mathbf {G}}),$$\end{document} which represents the adjacency relationships between its edges, is known as the para-line network and is represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S({\mathbf {G}}))$$\end{document}.

Estrado et al. conducted a thorough investigation into the properties of networks and established noteworthy boundaries for each network denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document}. These boundaries, which are crucial for understanding the structure and behavior of the networks, are outlined as follows:

Lemma 2.7

42 If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document} is any network other than a path network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_n,$$\end{document} then its FTMD \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} is at least 3.

Lemma 2.8

42 For any network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}}$$\end{document}, the MD \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} is always less than its FTMD \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document}.

Khular et al. also established the following key results:

Lemma 2.9

3 For any network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {G}},$$\end{document} let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{w_{1}, w_{2}\}\subset V({\mathbf {G}})$$\end{document} be an RS with MD being 2, then the degree of any vertex in this set does not exceed 3, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\mathbf {G}}(w_{1})\le 3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\mathbf {G}}(w_{2})\le 3$$\end{document}.

FTMD for the para-line network of n-sunlet network

Attaching n pendant edges to a cycle network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_n$$\end{document} produces the family of n-sunlet networks. The subdivision network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(S_n)$$\end{document} of n-sunlet network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_n$$\end{document} has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(S(S_n))$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_1, x_2,\ldots , x_{n}, a_1, a_2,\ldots , a_{n}, b_1, b_2,\ldots , b_{n}, y_1, y_2,\ldots , y_{n}\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(S(S_n))$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_{t}a_{t}: 1\le t\le n\} \cup$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{a_sx_{s+1}: 1 \le s \le n-1\} \cup$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_j b_j: 1\le j \le n\} \cup \{b_l y_l: 1\le l \le n\} \cup \{a_{n}x_{1}\}.$$\end{document} The subdivision network of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_n$$\end{document} for n= 8 is presented in Fig. 1.

Subdivision of n-sunlet network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(S_8)$$\end{document}.

Now to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document}, we need to convert the family of n-sunlet networks into their para-line network. The para-line network of n-sunlet network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document} has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{e_1, e_2,\ldots , e_{2n}, f_1, f_2,\ldots , f_{n}, g_1, g_2,\ldots , g_{n}\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{e_{t}e_{t+1}: 1 \le t \le 2n-1\}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup \{f_s e_{2s-1}: 1 \le s \le n\} \cup \{f_j e_{2j-2}: 2 \le j \le n\} \cup \{f_l g_l: 1\le l \le n\}\cup \{e_{2n} e_{1}, e_{2n} f_{1}\}.$$\end{document} The para-line network of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_n$$\end{document} for n=8 is presented in Fig. 2.Fig. 2Para-line graph of n-sunlet network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_8))$$\end{document}.

Para-line graph of n-sunlet network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_8))$$\end{document}.

The results about the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} is presented below.

Theorem 3.1

Let n\ge 3 be any integer, then FTMD of the para-line network of the n-sunlet network is

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \le 3, & \textrm{if}\ n = 3, 6; \\ \le 4, & else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

Proof

In order to prove the theorem, we examine the para-line network of the n-sunlet network, where n\ge 3. Our aim is to calculate an FTRS that contains at most 3 elements when n= 3 or 6, and at most 4 elements for all other values of n. We will explicitly construct such sets for these cases and show that they fulfill the fault-tolerant resolving condition. We take into account the structural symmetry and properties of the para-line network of the n-sunlet network while analyzing vertex distances to support our constructions. We will now proceed by reviewing all possible scenarios mentioned below.

Case 1: (If n is even)

Let n=4 and 6, take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}_{1}=\{e_1, e_2, e_5, e_6\} \subseteq V(L(S(S_4)))$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}_{2}=\{f_1, f_3, f_5\} \subseteq V(L(S(S_6)))$$\end{document} respectively. It is quite simple to verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}_{2}$$\end{document} are the FTRSs. Take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{e_{1}, e_{2}, e_{n+1}, e_{n+2}\}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\subseteq V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for all integers n \ge 8. For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(e_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (0, 1, n, n-1), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (\sigma -1, -2+\sigma , n-\sigma +1, 2-\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le n+1; \\ (1-\sigma +2n, 2-\sigma +2n, -n+\sigma -1,\\ -n+\sigma -2), & \textrm{if}\ n+2 \le \sigma \le 2n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(f_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (1, 2, n, n-1), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (-2+2\sigma , 2\sigma -3, 3-2\sigma +n, 4-2\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le \frac{n+2}{2}; \\ (3-2\sigma +2n, 4-2\sigma +2n, -2-n+2\sigma ,\\ -3-n+2\sigma ), & \textrm{if}\ \frac{n+4}{2} \le \sigma \le n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(g_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (2, 3, 1+n, n), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (-1+2\sigma , -2+2\sigma , 4-2\sigma +n, 5-2\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le \frac{n+2}{2} ; \\ (4-2\sigma +2n, 5-2\sigma +2n, -n+2\sigma -1,\\ -n+2\sigma -2), & \textrm{if}\ \frac{n+4}{2} \le \sigma \le n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

We may deduce from the aforementioned codes that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((g_{1}, g_{2})|W^{\prime})$$\end{document} has non-zero in two places in their 4-vector for different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{1}, g_{2} \in V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document}. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \le 4$$\end{document} for n ≠ 6 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_6))) \le 3$$\end{document}.

Case 2:

(If n is odd) Let n=3, take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{f_1, f_2, f_3\} \subseteq V(L(S(S_3)))$$\end{document}. It is quite simple to verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is the fault-tolerant resolving set. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_3))) \le 3$$\end{document}. Take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1, e_2, e_{n+1}, e_{n+2}\} \subseteq V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for all integers n \ge 5. For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(e_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (0, 1, n, -1+n), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (\sigma -1, \sigma -2, 1-\sigma +n, 2-\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le n+1; \\ (1-\sigma +2n, 2-\sigma +2n, -n+\sigma -1,\\ -n+\sigma -2), & \textrm{if}\ n+2 \le \sigma \le 2n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(f_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (1, 2, n, -1+n), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (-2+2\sigma , -3+2\sigma , -2\sigma +n+3, 4-2\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le \frac{n+1}{2}; \\ (n, n, 1, 1), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = \frac{n+3}{2}; \\ (-2\sigma +2n+3, -2\sigma +2n+4, -n+2\sigma -2,\\ -n+2\sigma -3), & \textrm{if}\ \frac{n+5}{2} \le \sigma \le n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

For the vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_\sigma$$\end{document}, the distance codes are

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(g_{\sigma }|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (2, 3, 1+n, n), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = 1; \\ (-1+2\sigma , -2+2\sigma , -2\sigma +n+4, 5-2\sigma +n), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le \sigma \le \frac{n+1}{2}; \\ (1+n, 1+n, 2, 2), & \textrm{if}\ \sigma = \frac{n+3}{2}; \\ (-2\sigma +2n+4, -2\sigma +2n+5, -1-n+2\sigma ,\\ -n+2\sigma -2), & \textrm{if}\ \frac{n+5}{2} \le \sigma \le n. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

We may deduce from the aforementioned codes that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((g_{1}, g_{2})|W^{\prime})$$\end{document} have non-zero in two places in their 4-vector for different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{1}, g_{2} \in V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document}. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \le 4$$\end{document} for n≠ 3.

□

Theorem 3.2

Let n\ge 3 be any integer, then FTMD of the para-line network of the n-sunlet network is

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \ge 3, & \textrm{if}\ n = 3, 6; \\ \ge 4, & else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

Proof

In order to determine the specified lower bounds, we examine the structure and symmetry of the para-line network within the n-sunlet network. This network displays significant symmetry, particularly for lower values of n, which complicates the differentiation of vertex pairs with a small number of landmarks. We move forward by reviewing all potential scenarios below.

Case 1:(n is even)

To prove our required result, suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))=3$$\end{document}. For n=4, we can verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((g_{1}, g_{2})|W^{\prime})$$\end{document} has zeros in at least two places in their 3-vector for different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{1}, g_{2} \in V(L(S(S_4)))$$\end{document}. So, there does not exist any FTRS. Now, for n\ge 8, all the possibilities of 3-vertex candidate FTRSs are analyzed below using the structural symmetry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,e_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s < t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2n},f_1)|W^{\prime})=(0,0,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,e_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s \le n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+1 \le t \le 2n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2n},f_1)|W^{\prime})=(0,0,1)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,f_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s \le n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2n},f_1)|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{*{20}l} (0,0,0), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le t \le \frac{n}{2}; \\ (0,0,1), & {else}. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document} This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s \le n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2n},f_1)|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{*{20}l} (0,0,0), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le t \le \frac{n}{2}; \\ (0,0,1), & else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document} This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+1 \le s \le n+2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le n-1$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2t+1},f_{t+1})|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{*{20}l} (0,1,0), & \textrm{if}\ 1 \le t \le \frac{n}{2}; \\ (1,0,0), & else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document} This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+1 \le s \le n+2$$\end{document} and t = n, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{1},f_1)|W^{\prime})=(1,0,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{e_1,e_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+3 \le s \le 2n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{*{20}l} (0,0,1), & \textrm{if}\ 2 \le t \le n-2; Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{f_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+4}{2} \le s \le n-1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+6}{2} \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (0,1,0), & \textrm{if}\ s = \frac{n+4}{2}; \\ (0,0,0), & \mathrm \ else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document} This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{f_1,f_s,f_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+2}{2} \le t \le n-2$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{n-4},f_{\frac{n-2}{2}})|W^{\prime})= (1,0,0)$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s < t \le \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2n},f_{1})|W^{\prime})= (1,0,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s \le \frac{n-2}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+2}{2} \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2(n-1)},f_{n})|W^{\prime})= (0,0,1)$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le s \le \frac{n-2}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+2}{2} \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{2(n-1)},f_{n})|W^{\prime})= (0,0,1)$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+2}{2} \le t \le n-2$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{n-4},f_{\frac{n-2}{2}})|W^{\prime})= (1,0,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{n+2}{2} \le t \le n-2$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{n-4},f_{\frac{n-2}{2}})|W^{\prime})= (1,0,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1 \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= (0,1,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n}{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1 \le t \le n$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= (0,1,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

According to the aforementioned absolute difference codes, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document} has no FTRS with a cardinality of 3. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \ge 4$$\end{document} for n ≠ 6.

Case 2: (If n is odd)

To prove our required result, suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))=3$$\end{document}. For n=3, we can verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((g_{1}, g_{2})|W^{\prime})$$\end{document} has zeros in at least two places in their 3-vector for different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{1}, g_{2} \in V(L(S(S_3)))$$\end{document}. So there does not exist any FTRS. Now, for n\ge 5, all the possibilities of 3-vertex candidate FTRSs are analyzed below using to the structural symmetry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n+1}{2}$$\end{document} and t = n, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= (0,1,0)$$\end{document}.
