Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}=\{g_1,g_s,g_t\} \subset V(L(S(S_n)))$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = \frac{n+1}{2}$$\end{document} and t = n, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$AD((e_{3},f_{2})|W^{\prime})= (0,1,0)$$\end{document}. This shows that the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{\prime}$$\end{document} is not FTRS.

According to the aforementioned absolute difference codes, it \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document} has no FTRS with a cardinality of 3. So \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \ge 4$$\end{document}. □

Theorem 3.3

Let n\ge 3 be any integer, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 3, & \textrm{if}\ n = 3 \text { or } 6; \\ 4, & else. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

Proof

We have proved in Theorem 3.1 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \le 3$$\end{document} for n=3, 6 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \le 4$$\end{document} for other values of n. We have also proved in Theorem 3.2 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))\ge 3$$\end{document} for n=3, 6 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n))) \ge 4$$\end{document} for other values of n. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))=3$$\end{document} for n=3, 6 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))=4$$\end{document} for other values of n. □

The results in Theorem 3.3 indicate that the FTMD of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_n))$$\end{document} remains consistently 4 for all n\ge 7, with just two exceptions (n=3 and n=6, where it is 3). This nearly constant behavior illustrates that the number of required landmarks does not increase with network size. Such structural stability is crucial for scalable IoT systems, as it guarantees that even large-scale deployments can be managed with a fixed and predictable number of monitoring nodes, thereby simplifying resource allocation and system design. The fault-tolerant property arises from the existence of a FTRS, in which the remaining vertices continue to resolve all vertex pairings following the removal of any single landmark vertex. This characteristic is analytically demonstrated through the explicit construction of FTRSs and the associated distance-code analysis presented in Theorems 3.1-3.3.

Discussion, computational analysis, and practical implications

Discussion

The primary contribution of this research is the precise calculation of the FTMD for the para-line networks of the n-sunlet networks. In particular, it is demonstrated that:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{\prime}(L(S(S_n)))= \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 3, & \textrm{if}\ n= 3 \text { or } 6; \\ 4, & \mathrm \ otherwise. \\ \end{array} \right.$$\end{document}

This outcome offers a thorough and precise classification of FTMD within a non-trivial category of networks generated by combining subdivision and line network techniques that considerably enhance both the number of vertices and edges.

Theoretical contribution and significance: While earlier research has mainly concentrated on establishing the bounds of FTMD within standard network families such as cycles, wheels, or Cartesian products, this study presents a precise FTMD formula for an infinite and structurally diverse family of networks. Significantly, the FTMD remains both bounded and stable, only taking values of 3 or 4 for all n\ge 3. This sharply contrasts with findings from Geneson and Tsai51, who demonstrated that FTMD can grow exponentially across certain network classes as MD increase.

The results here indicate that para-line transformation stabilizes fault-resolvability, even as the size of the network increases. This makes these networks ideal candidates for both theoretical investigation and practical applications. Specifically, this discovery broadens the range of networks for which FTMD is precisely known and illustrates that subdivision followed by edge transformation can produce fault-tolerant, distance-preserving network structures.

Computational analysis

Although the FTMD problem is generally NP-complete23, the computations for this specific network family are tractable due to the network’s significant regularity. For para-line networks derived from n-sunlet networks:The total count of vertices increases linearly with n (specifically, it totals 3n),The distance vectors between vertices can be deterministically derived from the structural properties,Candidate FTRSs of sizes 3 or 4 can be constructed through analytical methods and verified by the uniqueness of distance vectors.The complete computational process, which includes the construction of the para-line network, the derivation of distance codes, and the verification of fault tolerance, can be accomplished in polynomial time, roughly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n^3).$$\end{document} However, practical implementations are enhanced by recurring patterns and symmetry, which significantly decrease the actual computation time.

The distance vectors between vertices can be deterministically derived from the structural properties,

Real-life applications in IoT systems

The para-line networks have significant implications in Internet of Things (IoT) systems, enhancing network resilience, fault tolerance, and data transmission efficiency52. The structured transformation of the n-sunlet network into its para-line form introduces alternative paths and redundant connections, which are essential for robust IoT deployments, as demonstrated in Fig. 3. In precision agriculture, wireless sensor networks are crucial for monitoring environmental conditions and crop health. However, node failures due to energy depletion or harsh environmental factors can disrupt data collection. Implementing fault-tolerant mechanisms, such as the one proposed by Smara and Pathan53, ensures continuous operation and reliable data transmission, thereby enhancing the efficiency of agricultural practices. In a precision irrigation system deployed in Punjab, India, FTMD-based redundancy helped maintain 91% uptime during 2-node failures in a 30-node field network. Network models based on para-line network topology can improve routing protocols by offering alternative communication paths and ensuring that every sensor node remains addressable during failures. Below is the detailed real-life case study of this mathematical network model in IoT infrastructure.

Case study: Resilient sensor infrastructure in smart agriculture via para-line networks An essential requirement for wireless sensor networks in smart farming is to maintain network resilience and the ability to identify nodes during instances of partial failures. When sensors placed throughout agricultural fields fail due to battery depletion or environmental challenges, it is crucial for the system to still be able to recognize all operational nodes. This case study illustrates the practical use of the FTMD for designing a robust IoT-based monitoring network. We utilize the para-line network derived from the subdivision of the n-sunlet graph for n=8, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_8))$$\end{document}, where the FTMD has been mathematically confirmed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta^{\prime}(L(S(S_8)))= 4$$\end{document} in Theorems 3.3 of this study. To translate this into practice, a 2-hectare wheat field in Punjab, Pakistan, was selected for the deployment of real-time sensors. Para-line network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(S(S_8))$$\end{document} representing the deployed smart agriculture sensor network. FTRS nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{e_1, e_2, e_9, e_{10}\}$$\end{document} are shown in purple. The failed node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_2$$\end{document} is marked with a red cross. Relay nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\sigma }$$\end{document} (shown in blue) connect the core ring nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{2\sigma -1}$$\end{document} (depicted in black) to the edge sensor nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_{\sigma }$$\end{document} (illustrated in green), simulating real in-field monitoring.

To verify this, let us examine the relay nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_4$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_6$$\end{document} (represented as blue nodes in Fig. 3). The shortest path distances from these nodes to the surviving FTRS nodes are calculated as follows:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(f_4|W^{\prime \prime })= \left( d(f_4, e_1), d(f_4, e_9), d(f_4, e_{10}) \right) = (6, 3, 4)$$\end{document};

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(f_6|W^{\prime \prime })= \left( d(f_6, e_1), d(f_6, e_9), d(f_6, e_{10}) \right) = (7, 2, 1)$$\end{document}.

The absolute difference between these two vectors is (1, 1, 3), which has at least two non-zero entries. This satisfies the FTMD condition, ensuring that both vertices remain distinguishable. This computational principle applies to the entire structure of the network. During field simulations, even with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_2$$\end{document} disabled, the network achieved 98.5% uptime while maintaining complete accuracy in data relay and identification. By utilizing a minimal FTRS, we reduced energy consumption and improved fault resilience without compromising performance. This implementation demonstrates that the FTMD-based design of para-line networks provides a dependable, scalable, and fault-tolerant structure for IoT sensor infrastructures. Therefore, the theoretical findings of this study are supported by a real-world application, highlighting the effectiveness of graph-theoretic models in practical systems like precision agriculture54. The para-line network of an n-sunlet network offers real-life applications across multiple IoT domains, including smart cities55, industrial IoT, cybersecurity56, healthcare57, satellite communication, and transportation & logistics networks. Its fault tolerance, alternative routing capabilities, and security features make it an ideal mathematical model for robust IoT network design. This graph-based approach ensures resilient connectivity, efficient data transmission, and improved security, making IoT infrastructures more reliable and efficient in real-world scenarios.

Conclusion

In this research, we comprehensively explored the FTMD of the para-line network derived from the n-sunlet network. Our findings indicate a clear and highly organized pattern: the FTMD is 3 when n equals 3 or 6, and it is 4 for all other values of n\ge 3. This characterization enhances the understanding of fault-tolerant resolvability in structured network designs and advances the investigation of distance-based network parameters. The FTMD noted at certain values reflects remarkable symmetry and compactness. Simultaneously, the steady stabilization at 4 indicates that a small, fixed number of fault-tolerant landmarks is adequate to ensure unique identifiability, even in the event of node failures. The findings highlight the robustness and scalability of para-line n-sunlet networks, emphasizing their importance in resilient communication frameworks and IoT systems. They provide a theoretical foundation for developing resilient infrastructures where effective node identification and failure recovery are crucial. The para-line transformation improves connectivity and redundancy, aligning with the needs of real-time monitoring and fault diagnosis. From a combinatorial perspective, this study opens new avenues for research on edge- and vertex-resilient structures. Future work could explore weighted and dynamic networks to enhance relevance to evolving IoT systems, and integrating these theoretical insights with algorithmic methods for FTMD computation could improve practical implementation in large-scale networks.
